IDFA Artistic Director Isabel Arrate Fernandez On Festival’s Competition Lineups, “Huge” Interest In Elizabeth Holmes Doc, And AI In Documentaries

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 39th edition of IDFA – the world’s biggest nonfiction film festival – is underway in Amsterdam, gathering the documentary field’s top talents, Oscar seekers and emerging filmmakers with dynamic work.

“It’s hectic, all the last-minute things,” artistic director Isabel Arrate Fernandez told Deadline just before the kickoff. “From the lineup competitions to also the industry program we have, there are many exciting things going on.”

The opening night slot Friday evening goes to White Tiger Column, a world premiere directed by Robert Mentov, Dwight Jantzi, and Bryan Dickie that’s set within the resistance movement opposing Myanmar’s military-dominated regime. The festival calls it “a striking example of documentary cinema… The film offers a moving portrait of a courageous generation.”

White Tiger Column is programmed in International Competition, along with films from Morocco, Madagascar, Burkina Faso, Brazil, France, Germany, the Republic of Georgia, and other countries. An Incomplete Day in the History of Georgia 10.06.93, directed by Salomé Jashi, examines a critical moment for Georgia in 1993 when the country, recently freed from Soviet domination, debated measures to replace the Russian ruble. Considering Vladimir Putin’s attempt to reassert Russian dominance over much of the old USSR – including Ukraine – it’s a film with immediate relevance. Watch on Deadline

A baker’s dozen of features is programmed in the festival’s Envision section, which platforms films with bold stylistic choices. “The way we look at both of our competitions,” Arrate Fernandez notes, “they are of equal value… In Envision, we are really looking at filmmakers that are challenging documentary, are challenging the form.” Arrate Fernandez adds, “If we don’t have that, then how does a genre, how does cinema evolve?”

One of the Envision titles, Ragazzi al fiume, examines the experience of three young Afghan immigrants to Switzerland. The IDFA program notes the documentary directed by Pablo Briones “gradually evolves into an observational portrait of isolation, loss, and the longing for a peaceful future.”

Fellow Envision Competition documentary The Black Box Is Orange focuses on the Israeli town of Shlomi that “was built on land belonging to the destroyed Palestinian village of Al-Bassa, near the Lebanese border,” according to the IDFA program. “Beginning with the Nakba mass displacement of Palestinians in 1948, its residents were forced out and citizens of the newly established state of Israel were settled there. All traces of the previous inhabitants were to be destroyed, including all Palestinian archives.”

That film is directed by Yuula Benivolski, an Israeli Jewish filmmaker born in Canada and raised in the village of Shlomi. (Two years ago, she posted a video on YouTube discussing her decision to renounce her Israeli citizenship. “It wasn’t a decision I made lightly… Yes, I did it in response to Israel’s horrific assault on Gaza,” she explained, before adding, “Whether Israel realizes it or not, all this death harms its own people as well.”).

More than 250 films are programmed across the festival’s sections. Among them are some of the strongest contenders for Oscars, including To Hold a Mountain, the Sundance winner directed by Biljana Tutorov and Petar Glomazić; Once Upon a Time in Harlem, directed by David Greaves and based upon material shot by his father, William Greaves (this festival is separately screening a large selection of William Greaves’s work); Musk, directed by Alex Gibney; Closure, directed by Michał Marczak, and You Can See Everything, the box office sensation about Elizabeth Holmes that’s directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim. Do they know the imprisoned Theranos founder in the Netherlands? Maybe so, maybe not, but enthusiasm for the film has certainly crossed the Atlantic.

Interest in You Can See Everything “has been huge,” Arrate Fernandez affirms. “The festival screenings sold out very quickly… It’s definitely generated some attention, to put it mildly.”

IDFA has become an increasingly important stop for filmmakers in Oscar contention, in part because of the plethora of Academy documentary branch voters who are expected to attend. (“It’s difficult to count them, I can tell you,” Arrate Fernandez notes. “Let’s see.”). Last year’s winner in IDFA’s International Competition, A Fox Under a Pink Moon, has become a leading contender for Oscar consideration this year. (That film’s protagonist and co-director, the young artist Soraya Akhlaghi, designed this year’s IDFA’s poster and contributed artwork used in the festival trailer. Watch it below).

Arrate Fernandez mentions other films in the 2026 IDFA lineup making a strong bid for Oscar recognition.

“The winner of Cannes, Rehearsals for a Revolution; Traces, a Ukrainian film talking about sexual assault as a war crime against women; Closure, NAZA… there are many interesting films there that I hope will make it,” she says. “Even though they are not the films with the bigger budgets and everything for their campaigning, I really hope they get far.”

14th, another top Oscar contender that will screen at IDFA, is among the documentaries that make use of AI – in the case of Ava DuVernay’s film, to animate vintage photographs. For the first time ever, the artistic director tells us, IDFA asked filmmakers to complete a form indicating if they had incorporated AI into their film’s production.

“Last year, we noticed that sometimes we were wondering while watching films and now we have this, we can go back and see what they filled out just to understand,” Arrate Fernandez comments. “But also, it’s part of the conversations that are taking place with regards to how to use [AI], what is acceptable? There are so many questions, so I think that really [speaks] to conversations that are being held within the industry.”

To that end, IDFA will be hosting two panel discussions on AI, part of a robust lineup of industry talks. A panel titled “Fighting Back – Cinema as an act of resistance?” will feature Oscar winner Laura Poitras, who is bringing They’re Here to IDFA, a short film she directed with Rachel Lauren Mueller that examines the fatal ICE surge in Minneapolis. Fellow panelists include Sofía Bordenave, director of Forest Up in the Mountain, and Mohanad Yaqubi, director of Revolutionaries Never Die. Fernandez is slated to moderate a conversation with this year’s IDFA Guest of Honor, filmmaker Tatiana Huezo.

This is the second year of Arrate Fernandez’s leadership as artistic director. Last year, she faced questions after IDFA revealed it would not invite to the festival what it considered to be Israeli state-supported organizations, such as DocAviv. On its website, IDFA spells out its position under a section titled “Principles and guidelines” governing the 2026 festival.

“For films, makers, and organizations from countries where freedom of expression is under pressure or human rights are violated, it is essential to assess and investigate their independence carefully, alongside the artistic selection process,” IDFA writes. “In this way, we aim to prevent governments and organizations involved in and/or suspected of serious human rights violations from gaining a platform at IDFA.”

The IDFA policy statement continues, “Films, makers, and organizations are never excluded on the basis of their origin and/or nationality, but rather on the basis of any possible relationship with regimes or organizations held internationally accountable for serious human rights violations.”

Arrate Fernandez tells Deadline, “We also really looked at what we did last year, how we’re implementing things, what are our internal processes. And basically, for… a film work or a film project, everything just went through the regular route of being evaluated by our different teams of programmers on the creative criteria. And the moment that films make it through the different phases and end up on a short list, then in cases of countries… that are known for human rights violations, we check the independence of this film or of this project in the sense of also going about checking credits, who are financiers. At the same time, it’s not a blanket approach in the sense of, ‘Oh, this financier, it’s out.’ We also look at the film, so we still have our case-by-case approach.”

She continued, “Then in terms of accreditations… we have the similar line in the sense of having this case-by-case approach.”

American Israeli filmmaker Rachel Leah Jones (Advocate) will be presenting her new project, Podium (You Have Three Minutes), at IDFA Forum, the event’s pitching, co-financing and co-production market for nonfiction and new media projects. Logline: “What does resistance sound like when delivered from the parliamentary podium? Since Israel’s founding, Palestinian lawmakers have taken the mic to appeal, demand, denounce, and warn — articulating a powerful counter-history of the state itself.”

Avi Mograbi, described in the IDFA program as “one of Israel’s most distinguished filmmakers,” heads to the festival for the Dutch premiere of his short documentary Wild Asparagus (Halayon), which bowed at the Venice Film Festival. In Collapse, Israeli director Anat Even returns to Kibbutz Nir Oz, where she lost her home and friends in the Hamas sneak attack of October 7, 2023. She films what remains there and also attempts to enter Gaza, documenting “a gathering of settlers who plan to build in Gaza and proclaim this by chanting, ‘Occupy, expel, settle,’” the festival program reads. “An introspective and revealing film essay on loss, grief, revenge, dehumanization and indifference.” (The program description includes this note: “This film contains content on potentially sensitive topics.”).

The production company for Collapse is listed as France.

“You won’t find films that have as a production country Israel” in this year’s festival, Arrate Fernandez indicates, “but there are Israeli filmmakers. The thing is also that we don’t ask people for their nationality… We have several films that are taking place in Israel and others that are taking place in Palestine or in the Occupied Territories.”

Just about every corner of the globe is reflected in the festival lineup. IDFA runs through November 22, a chance for documentary filmmakers and lovers of the form to gather in person in one of the world’s great cities.

Says Arrate Fernandez, “In a world where storytelling is being formatted by decision makers, AI, algorithm, numbers, I think it’s important to treasure these spaces.”

You can watch the festival’s trailer here, featuring the contributions of the team behind A Fox Under a Pink Moon, including artist, protagonist and co-director Soraya Akhlaghi, co-director Mehrdad Oskouei, and composer Afshin Azizi

Strategy Inc (NASDAQ: MSTR) has witnessed one of its biggest corrections in recent years. After hitting an all-time high near $540 in late 2025 and rallying above $200 during the first half of 2026, the stock recently regained momentum and retained the critical $100 mark.

While MSTR remains one of Wall Street’s most closely watched Bitcoin-related stocks, the recent sell-off has significantly compressed its valuation despite continued treasury expansion and growing institutional participation. The company’s long-term outlook continues to depend on Bitcoin’s performance, treasury growth, and investor demand for Bitcoin-linked equities.

In this MSTR price prediction for 2026-2040, we analyze the stock’s recent performance, treasury growth, analyst outlook, and whether Strategy Inc can recover from current levels and eventually reach the $500 milestone.

Strategy’s Inc valuation is increasingly driven by its Bitcoin treasury. With more than 843,000 BTC on its balance sheet, Bitcoin price movements, BTC Yield, and treasury growth directly influence MSTR’s value.

As the largest corporate Bitcoin holder, MSTR has become one of the market’s primary Bitcoin-linked equities.

Wall Street increasingly values Strategy Inc based on its ability to grow its Bitcoin treasury rather than its software business. The company prioritizes BTC Yield, Bitcoin-per-share growth, and capital allocation, supported by expanded equity programs, preferred securities offerings, and continued access to capital markets.

Investors continue to monitor financing costs, share issuance, and market liquidity, as these factors affect the company’s ability to fund additional Bitcoin purchases. As more public companies adopt Bitcoin treasury strategies, Strategy’s Inc ability to maintain its scale and grow Bitcoin exposure per share is likely to remain a key driver of MSTR’s valuation.

Strategy stock (MSTR)  has recovered sharply after finding strong buying interest around its lower demand zone, triggering a sustained rebound toward the upper resistance area. However, the rally lost momentum near the major supply zone, leading to a pullback and a period of consolidation.

The stock faces immediate resistance near $125–$130, where a sustained breakout could strengthen recovery momentum toward $140–$150. A broader supply zone remains around $165–$200. On the downside, initial support lies near $110–$105, while the major demand zone is positioned around $100. Holding above the immediate support could help MSTR stabilize and attempt another recovery. However, a breakdown below $100 may expose the stock to renewed selling pressure toward the lower demand area.

Coinpedia forecasts MSTR to trade between $100 and $250 in 2026, with an average price target of $180.

Strategy reported its third-quarter 2026 earnings on October 29, posting $19.53 billion in net income, $20.91 billion in operating income, and diluted EPS of $44.75. However, revenue came in at $125.4 million, below analysts’ expectations of $128.03 million. The results were largely driven by a $20.91 billion fair-value gain on Bitcoin holdings, which reached 848,517 BTC as of October 25. Strategy also reported a $5.07 billion USD reserve, providing approximately 3.4 years of coverage for preferred dividends and interest obligations.

Investors searching for the MSTR stock dividend should note that Strategy does not pay a regular dividend on its common stock. Its preferred security, STRC, carries a 12% annualized dividend rate, with daily accruals beginning in November 2026. The aggregate November dividend is $1.00 per STRC share.

Strategy is facing a critical period as MSCI prepares to remove MSTR from its global equity indexes after the November 30 market close. The decision could trigger passive fund selling, with estimates pointing to approximately 13 million shares potentially affected. More importantly, the exclusion raises questions about Strategy’s ability to maintain its Bitcoin accumulation strategy through equity financing.

Bitcoin’s decline below Strategy’s average acquisition cost of $75,456 adds another challenge. The company held 850,364 BTC as of November 8, following its latest purchase of 1,036 BTC for $81.9 million. However, its latest filing also showed $188.3 million allocated to repurchasing STRC preferred stock, compared with $81.9 million directed toward Bitcoin purchases. Its $4.77 billion cash reserve provides an additional liquidity buffer.

Meanwhile, JPMorgan reported a 36% increase in its MSTR shareholding during Q3, although its filing also disclosed substantial put-option exposure. This makes the development less straightforward as a bullish institutional signal.

Coinpedia’s MSTR price forecasts are based on a combination of historical stock performance, Bitcoin market cycles, treasury growth trends, institutional analyst forecasts, and technical market structure. Since Strategy trades as both a Bitcoin-linked equity and a Nasdaq-listed company, both crypto and traditional market factors were considered.

Strategy Inc remains one of the strongest Bitcoin-linked equities in public markets, with its long-term performance largely dependent on Bitcoin adoption and treasury growth. While short-term volatility and financing risks may continue to pressure the stock, the company’s aggressive accumulation strategy and growing institutional interest could support higher valuations over the coming years. Coinpedia expects MSTR to remain a high-conviction Bitcoin play for long-term investors.

According to Coinpedia’s MSTR price prediction, the stock could reach a maximum target of $1,000 by 2030 if Bitcoin adoption continues to expand and Strategy maintains its treasury growth strategy.

3. Can MSTR benefit from institutional Bitcoin adoption?

Yes, growing institutional demand for Bitcoin and Bitcoin-linked equities could support MSTR over the long term. Increased corporate adoption and treasury expansion may further strengthen the investment case.

4. What are the major risks for MSTR investors?

Bitcoin volatility, share dilution from capital raises, higher interest rates, and regulatory changes remain the biggest risks. A prolonged decline in Bitcoin prices could significantly pressure MSTR’s valuation.

MSTR offers investors indirect exposure to Bitcoin through a publicly traded stock. Its long-term outlook depends on Bitcoin prices, treasury growth, institutional demand, and the company’s capital allocation strategy.

Palantir adds three UK police forces to its beat with £10.1M deal

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Five-year contract follows London setback as former Met chief Cressida Dick emerges as a paid adviser

Three UK police forces led by Cambridgeshire Constabulary have awarded Palantir a £10.1 million contract after London Mayor Sadiq Khan blocked a separate deal with the Metropolitan Police.

The five-year agreement covers Bedfordshire Police, Cambridgeshire Constabulary, and Hertfordshire Constabulary, despite warnings from campaigners and politicians about dependence on the supplier.

A contract award notice says the east of England police forces expect the Palantir platform to provide "an enterprise-wide data integration and analytics capability, enabling the secure ingestion, linking, and analysis of data from multiple systems and sources across the policing environment." The contract runs until October 2031, with the estimated value covering all three forces.

News of the award coincided with revelations that former Metropolitan Police commissioner Dame Cressida Dick has worked as a paid adviser to Palantir since September 2025.

A Palantir spokesperson told the Bureau of Investigative Journalism: "Dame Cressida is advising us on how our software could help improve the delivery of services in the public sector, drawing on more than 40 years of public service in a variety of roles."

Dick said her role was limited to advising on how such products could assist public services. She said she had never lobbied or advocated for Palantir, or introduced the company to her contacts.

Dick led the Metropolitan Police, the UK's largest force, between 2017 and 2022. She left after Khan said he had lost confidence in her. She later said she was "intimidated" into leaving.

Separately, Khan stepped in to block the Met from awarding a £50 million contract to Palantir. His office cited serious concerns about the procurement process. Palantir later sued the Met, but the case was settled in October.

The new police contract award follows a report from MPs warning of vendor lock-in to US suppliers, including Palantir. It said: "Our view that Palantir's increasing presence across the public sector represents an unacceptable point of weakness is not ideologically motivated or driven by concerns about the quality of their products.

"The government should retain the ability to pick and choose individual suppliers and safeguard against the risk of vendor lock-in and debilitating dependencies, particularly in areas of critical national importance such as healthcare and national security infrastructure." ®

What we know about the global Microsoft outage

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch: What we know about the Microsoft global outage overnight

A major global Microsoft outage has caused problems, or in some cases disabled entire services, including emails and artificial intelligence products.

Microsoft has said the outage was not caused by a cyber-security incident, but efforts to fix the issues have so far been largely unsuccessful.

Here's what we know about the services affected and what went wrong.

Schools, GPs and businesses hit by global Microsoft outage as company scrambles to solve issue

Microsoft 365 Services, including Teams, Outlook and Microsoft's artificial intelligence software Copilot, have been affected by "connectivity problems".

Customers are reporting varying levels of access - many have not been able to send or receive emails, view certain files or details on their calendar apps.

Some users have been able to use Teams and are using messaging and online meeting functions without issues.

More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps according to Microsoft's website.

The full scale of the outage has not been confirmed, but monitoring website Downdetector had 140,000 people from 74 countries report issues with Outlook on Thursday evening.

The real figure affected is likely much higher because Downdetector relies primarily on users self-reporting.

Microsoft 365 is also used by companies worldwide and is expected to have a knock-on effect to other services.

Tech giants OpenAI and Anthropic have both reported problems as a result of the outage.

ChatGPT-maker OpenAI said it was investigating an issue affecting its connections to Microsoft's services, which has caused some tools to not work as expected.

From city governments in the US to Australian spy agencies, disruption across the globe has been widespread.

Courts from Los Angeles to Texas warned that communications might be delayed.

In Canada, the public broadcaster CBC reported that a healthcare network running several hospitals in Toronto was experiencing email issues, but that patient care was not affected.

The government of Japan also said its shared IT platform had been disrupted.

Across the UK, GP surgeries, pharmacies, police and schools have been affected by the outage.

Some GP surgeries have urged patients not to submit messages via their website while the problem remains, while several large police forces are urging members of the public to call if they want to make a report.

Microsoft has ruled out a cyber-attack and attributed the ongoing outage to "elevated load" on its infrastructure.

The term refers to when servers are handling a much higher volume of stress than they usually do.

In the case of the Microsoft outage, a large number of servers became "unexpectedly unavailable", the company said, which sent added traffic to remaining servers, straining these.

On Friday afternoon, Microsoft said it had seen "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe and improvement in North America.

The company added it is focused on "addressing the remaining areas of disruption" and warned "some users may still experience intermittent issues as recovery actions continue".

In an earlier statement, the tech giant had said that its attempted fixes were "not taking as expected".

Australian cyber-security expert Troy Hunt told the BBC the outage could have far-reaching consequences.

"Microsoft is a massive part of the internet backbone and reminds us how critical email still is.

"Not only does this outage affect many people's ability to work but also things like the ability to communicate about appointments, purchase things that need verification, create or access accounts and services," he said.

Gergely Orosz, a former leading software engineer at several major tech companies including Skype and Uber, said this could be the "most disruptive" business email outage "of all time".

He said it was still difficult to know just how many people have been affected by the disruption, but added he "cannot recall" an outage over business email ever taking this long to return to normal.

Paul Spain from New Zealand-based advisory firm Gorilla Technology said that with e-mails and services offline, companies have practically "lost a business day", which is "frustrating" users".

Have you or your business been affected by the outage?

The catch in crypto’s Anthropic IPO rush: lock up your money and you could still miss out

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Anthropic IPO token buyers face uncertain share allocations, locked funds and fees of up to 12% ahead of its Nasdaq debut.

Crypto investors seeking Anthropic IPO tokens can commit money and still get a refund instead of an allocation.

Kraken, Bitget, and BigONE disclose subscription terms that make delivery dependent on available shares, while successful fills carry additional costs.

The issue became more immediate on Nov. 12 when BigONE opened indications of interest and warned users they could receive a full allocation, a partial allocation, or none. Kraken's Anthropic IPO access terms carry the same three possible outcomes, while Bitget says its commitment caps do not represent the shares it has secured.

Those warnings come ahead of Anthropic's expected Nov. 18 US-time pricing and Nov. 19 Nasdaq debut. Bloomberg News reported on Nov. 11, citing people familiar with the matter, that orders already exceeded the available shares multiple times.

The venues' terms make clear that money can become unavailable while an allocation is determined, and receiving tokens can mean paying above the offering price without acquiring direct shareholder rights.

Allocation decides what investors receive. Select a venue to see how money control, charges and expected delivery differ.

Kraken reserves funds. You can cancel free before the communicated cutoff; after it, the request becomes binding.

Bitget public commitments cannot be canceled. Funds stay locked; token delivery is expected before the regular U.S. session on IPO day.

The published VIP cutoff has passed. Commitments cannot be canceled; delivery is expected after the IPO and unallocated funds are expected back within hours of allocation confirmation, with delays possible.

BigONE uses cumulative subscription tiers and charges only on allocated shares. Full, partial or zero allocation remains possible.

Submitting money secures a place in the allocation process; the received tokens and refund depend on the result.

In a June 12 notice, Bybit said xStocks could not deliver the underlying SpaceX assets, leaving the exchange with no allocation.

Anthropic's Nov. 10 preliminary prospectus offers 825 million Class A shares at an estimated $70 to $78 each. About 801.4 million are company shares and 23.6 million are being sold by existing holders, whose sale proceeds do not go to Anthropic.

Bloomberg News reported that the public offering could total up to $64 billion. A separate $26 billion Class N private placement would bring the combined amount to as much as $90 billion.

The published charges apply only to the amount allocated:

Kraken reserves money when a user submits interest and charges it only if an allocation is received, and reserved funds remain unavailable until allocation or release. Its cancellation right ends at a communicated cutoff, after which the submission becomes binding.

Supported stablecoin submissions are converted to dollars, and refunds arrive in dollars.

Bitget's restriction says users cannot cancel submitted commitments or trade, transfer, or withdraw locked funds. Its fee is charged by the partner fund, broker, or placement channel and is included in the commitment.

The venue expects remaining funds to be refunded within hours of allocation confirmation, while warning that arrival can be delayed and processing extended if issuance or allocation arrangements fail.

Bitget's published VIP window was scheduled to end Nov. 11, while its public window is scheduled to end at 08:00 UTC on the day before the IPO.

The higher VIP fee does not remove allocation uncertainty, and the expected three-to-five-working-day delivery means those tokens may arrive after the stock's first trading day.

BigONE's window runs through Nov. 18 at 12:00 UTC. Its notice promises refunds if the platform receives no allocation, plus 6% annualized compensation for the time each subscription's funds were committed.

Payments below 1 USDT per subscription are excluded, and the payment schedule will be announced separately.

Using the preliminary range's $78 upper endpoint as an illustration, a 5% surcharge yields an acquisition cost of $81.90 per unit, while a 12% surcharge yields $87.36.

Assuming a token tracks one share without price divergence, the underlying price would need to rise 5% or 12%, respectively, from $78 just to match those acquisition costs, before any later trading charges.

Tottenham wonderkid and £16m Everton star on Scotland radar as Sebastien Pocognoli confirms approach

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sebastien Pocognoli has revealed he has made approaches to Tottenham youngster Archie Gray and Everton midfielder Hayden Hackney about representing Scotland as he looks to strengthen his international options.

The Belgian head coach is determined to identify talented players with Scottish connections who could potentially represent the national team, and has confirmed that both Premier League prospects feature on his recruitment radar.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Gray and Hackney have represented England at youth level but are eligible to play for Scotland, although neither has committed to switching their international allegiance. Pocognoli admits he has already established contact with the pair but insists any decision will ultimately have to come from the players themselves.

His predecessor, Steve Clarke, previously attempted to persuade the likes of Elliot Anderson and Harvey Barnes to represent Scotland, without success. However, the country has enjoyed greater luck in other cases, with Scott McTominay and Che Adams going on to become influential figures after opting to play for the Tartan Army.

Pocognoli is currently satisfied with the depth available to him as Scotland prepare for their final Nations League fixtures against North Macedonia and Switzerland. Nevertheless, he believes expanding the pool of eligible talent must remain an ongoing priority.

Speaking about his efforts to identify potential recruits, Pocognoli said: “I am busy with everything. We know which players are eligible to represent the country one day. It’s won't come from me, it has to come from them to show it. I try to anticipate everything. The best way is to be proactive and creative in certain situations.

“The depth of the squad is already big without these other names. I’m very happy with it because we have a good double-up and triple-up in each position. But it’s part of my role, for sure [to find players]. Not only for me but for other people working at the Scottish FA. We are on it. We also have a lot of good players in the Under-21s and that’s the first step for me, for the future.

“But yeah, I have had direct or indirect contact (with Gray and Hackney). When I go to the direct contact it’s because I feel it’s a first positive approach. So yeah, I’ve done it with many players. I’ll do my best to bring them.”

Gray and Hackney have different international backgrounds, with Pocognoli acknowledging that any potential switch would require patience.

Gray, the son of former Scotland striker Andy Gray, also has family links to Scottish football through Leeds United legends Frank and Eddie Gray, both of whom represented the national side. The Tottenham midfielder has featured for England at every youth level but has yet to make his senior debut.

Hackney, meanwhile, previously represented Scotland at Under-21 level before switching his international allegiance to England.

Pocognoli believes the age of the two players makes them particularly interesting prospects but insists there is no immediate pressure for either to make a decision.

“With Barnes it was different. I knew before my first camp that he was different,” he said. “The two other lads [Gray and Hackney] are young lads. It’s a long way [away]. But if you wait too long, it might not be the right moment. In a year’s time, we will see. Right now, we have players in the squad who are performing well.

“It will always be up to them [current players] to be the priority, because we’ve built things and we’ve had time with the ones who are here right now. But we are on it. We are always proactive in all of these situations. What is very exciting about those two names is that they are young players.

“That’s interesting. We have to look and manage the situation. But it won’t be a definitive choice from them - because we weren’t proactive. That has to be clear. We are proactive.

“The best way to convince players is to show good football, show them they can come to us, enjoy it and be successful. They have to feel when they leave a top club that they arrive here into top conditions and a top environment.

“That’s the best publicity we can offer, to show good football. Yeah [McTominay is an example] but there are many.”

Brad Pitt’s New Thriller Is Officially a Streaming Hit After Being Removed From Over 2,000 Theaters

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

One of the fall's biggest sleeper hits is finally available on the PVOD market, and as expected, it has emerged as an instant success. The movie received a "Verified Hot" 95% audience score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes, which foreshadowed its home-video potential. Movies with higher audience scores on aggregator websites tend to perform better on digital and streaming platforms. In recent months, movies such as Jason Statham's The Beekeeper and the World War II thriller Pressure have exemplified this theory.

The new survival thriller stars Brad Pitt and was directed by David Ayer. The two previously worked together more than a decade ago on the fan-favorite WWII action film Fury. Ayer has since shown a knack for making crowd-pleasers, although his career took a hit after the critically panned Suicide Squad. Ayer bounced back with The Beekeeper, which he followed up a year later with another Statham vehicle, A Working Man.

The two Statham films now hold "Verified Hot" 92% and 87% audience scores on Rotten Tomatoes, respectively. Ayer's latest film has outperformed them both. Pitt, on the other hand, was coming off the blockbuster sports drama F1, which grossed around $630 million worldwide on the back of a 97% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes.

His new movie with Ayer, of course, is Heart of the Beast. The film premiered in more than 3,400 theaters nationwide, and is still playing in roughly 1,100 locations as we speak. It was able to exceed expectations at the box office, grossing nearly $190 million worldwide against a reported budget of $82 million. It remains to be seen if the movie has the legs to pass the $200 million mark.

Now that it's available to rent and purchase at home, you can expect it to be removed from hundreds more theaters this coming week. This will no doubt affect its box-office performance, but the movie can take solace in a terrific debut on digital. According to FlixPatrol, Heart of the Beast is currently the number-one movie domestically on iTunes, having overtaken Spider-Man: Brand New Day, Verity, and Resident Evil immediately after its debut. Later this month, Pitt will star in The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, the spin-off of Once Upon a Time in Hollywood. The new movie is being given a two-week run in IMAX locations ahead of its Netflix release in December. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Breakfast News: What NVDA’s CEO Exit Means for You

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Huang built a company with the products, people, and customers to outlast him, and we expect Nvidia to come through this.

Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia's (NVDA -4.34%) chief executive amid controversies over his personal conduct and questions about whether Nvidia's chips reached China through a customer in Singapore.

His exit followed a week of reports about his personal life. The first concerned an affair with an executive at Megaspeed, one of Nvidia's customers in Singapore. U.S. officials suspected the executive of spying for China, which China denied. On Wednesday, a second relationship, with one of Huang's direct reports, came to light. The board met that night, and the company announced Huang's resignation the following morning.

We think investors are right to take Huang's departure seriously. In our view, though, the sell-off prices in more damage than the business has shown so far.

If you've followed Nvidia for any length of time, you know how central Huang has been. He co-founded the company in 1993 and led it for all 33 years. His leadership structure was also known for being unusually flat, as he had 33 direct reports at last count, according to CNBC.

Huang pushed Nvidia as an innovator and technologist. He launched CUDA (software that lets developers program Nvidia's chips) in 2006 and boldly committed the company to artificial intelligence (AI) computing long before it became a mainstream market. He also managed Nvidia's largest customer relationships himself and represented the company in Washington on export rules for China.

That is a lot for one person to hand off, let alone overnight, and Nvidia has no named successor to take it on. For now, CFO Colette Kress, who has worked alongside Huang since 2013 and has joined him on every earnings call, will serve as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement.

We understand why the market reacted so sharply. A company that leaned this heavily on one person's judgment now has to make its next big decisions without him, and under federal scrutiny. Analyst Dan Ives estimates Nvidia is worth 25% to 30% less without Huang, and some discount is warranted. With shares dropping to $148.80 in Thursday trading and closing at around $166, the market has already applied much of it.

What reassures us is that Huang's exit on its own is unlikely to change Nvidia's revenue much over the next two years. Demand for its chips is still growing quickly. Last quarter, revenue reached $96.2 billion, up 106% from a year earlier, and the company guided to $108 billion for the quarter that ended in October. By any measure, that's a business in good health.

Chips take years to design, so the products Nvidia will sell through 2027 were planned while Huang was still in charge, and customers have already ordered many of them. Even if regulators cut off sales routed through Singapore, we estimate Nvidia would lose no more than about 5% of its revenue.

A broader crackdown would cost more, and the investigations will help decide whether one comes.

We are concerned about a few things and expect volatility ahead.

This Ford sculptor has carved cars out of clay for 40 years. AI and VR just cut his work time in half

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

John “Rex” Allen is 68, wears cowboy boots, a black baseball cap, and he’s spent four decades building life-size cars out of clay. These days he builds inside a virtual-reality headset, too.

He does his work upstairs at Ford Motor Company’s gleaming new headquarters in Dearborn, Michigan, where the bird sounds in the yard aren’t even real. Just over four miles away, the 109-year-old Rouge Plant looks much the way it did in the 1920s, except with 3D-printed parts patching the factory together and a “Green Roof” overhead that is one of the world’s largest.

The new Ford World HQ opened in November 2025 as a 21st-century expression of the Ford Rouge logic, according to Ford Land CEO Jim Dobleske. World HQ stacks modern teams vertically the way Henry Ford stacked steel and assembly at the Rouge. It used to take 30 minutes to move a car from one end of the old design center to the other; now it takes “just over three minutes.” The birdsong in the courtyard is piped in, predator calls included, to keep Michigan’s ever-present geese away from workers wolfing down their lunches. “Those are not real birds,” Dobleske said, “that’s all fake.”

Fortune visited Ford in 1947 to document the “rebirth” of the company, when founder Henry Ford died and his grandson, Henry Ford II, was leading the postwar modernization of the iconic American manufacturer. Nearly eight decades later, Detroit as a whole is 13 years on from its historic bankruptcy process, but General Motors was the only member of the “Big Three” auto firms to post a net profit in 2025.

All three OEMs, or original equipment manufacturers, announced major pullbacks in their electric-vehicle strategies in 2025 as the federal government scrapped subsidies, prompting Ford CEO Jim Farley to (accurately) predict that the EV market would be cut in half as a result. In December, Ford announced about $19.5 billion in charges and pivoted to another rebirth: a race against the calendar.

Upstairs at Ford World HQ, Allen is looking at his clay cars, his monitors, his plastic models and his virtual reality glasses.

The Ford modeling studio is massive, with ceilings 25 to 30 feet high and a wide-open, blank space that feels like an empty Hollywood studio, awaiting a Gilmore Girls set or a replica of the Titanic.

Allen, with his thin white mustache complementing his mane of white hair, is doing what he’s always done: building cars out of clay.

Like most Michiganders who still work in auto manufacturing, Allen has an unimpressed air about his remarkable, unusual career. Asked how he joined the ranks of elite auto sculptors, he explains that he felt forced into it by fear of automation. “I used to be a machinist, and then it started becoming robotic, so I got out of that business real quick.” He reasoned that he’d always been a “bit of an artist,” and in high school loved comic books and cars and ran a side hustle airbrushing the “Rat Fink” mascot and other Kustom Kulture motifs onto T-shirts for other kids. Sort of a Mickey Mouse by way of R. Crumb, Rat Fink was a neon-green rodent mascot that exemplified the proto-punk vibes of Southern California car culture of the 1960s, but Allen said his tastes also ran a bit less gonzo — he used to carve Disney characters out of wood and clay for extra credit in art class, along with watercolors and oil paintings for extra credit in art class. “I made good money in high school,” Allen shrugs. “I loved doing that stuff.”

He didn’t set out to become a clay-man, either. “Somebody told me they created cars out of clay,” he explains, “which I had no idea of, so he introduced me to a guy that owned a business. And I mean, I fell right into it.” Allen recalled that he “tried it once” and the results spoke for themselves. He was 26 and soon found himself working 100 hours a week, pulling down roughly $100,000 a year.

Allen wasn’t in-house at Ford, then. At that time, Detroit had a dense web of small model shops that handled overflow work for the Big 3 — Ford, General Motors and Chrysler (now Stellantis), along with their suppliers — and Allen worked at a half-dozen of them. Work was steady. “You could grab a job any day of the week in one of these places,” he said, recalling that he worked on the original concept for the Dodge Viper at a Chrysler satellite studio. He’s now been at Ford for 24 years.

So, why clay? Allen and his colleagues, Nick Navarro, Shawn Fairall and Michael Smith, explained that it’s been that way for nearly a century. “Having the physical property in front of you to have the conversation with multiple people has always been a key part of the process,” Fairall said. “There’s always kind of this general misconception that computers have taken over car design,” Fairall said. “Clay has never really gone away.”

Smith, who leads research and development for Ford’s design team, always describes it to interns the same way. “We don’t sell pretty pictures of cars,” he said. “We sell vehicles.” He said a director might look at the clay and say, “you need to pull this back three more inches.”

It used to take about two weeks for one modeler to sculpt one full-size model, Allen said, and every change meant more weeks of work to do by hand. About six years ago, that changed with virtual reality and 3D printing. Now Allen can knock out a design’s theme in five days.

Allen said he’s seen a lot of upgrades in 40-odd years. First came “sweeps,” templates that modelers could drag along a car’s side. “That was a wow factor there for a while,” Allen said. Then came machines that could map the body, cut pieces out of it, and eventually shape an entire side. Now the team can rough out a car in clay, scan it in about 20 minutes, put on a headset to refine the shape digitally, and have a mill carve out the new surface over a few shifts, then continue working the surface by hand for the finishing touches. They call it hybrid sculpting. “This one is leaps and bounds,” Allen said, “further than anything we’ve ever done.”

“Because of technology, we’ve reduced the people over the years, so there’s less and less of us,” Allen said, adding, “we still have the same amount of work.” He said he thought this “virtual reality way of working would help keep us employed, basically, and it seems like it has, because we’re able to flip more designs now because of it.”

Those saved weeks matter because of what Ford is trying to pull off.

So what is the shape of the new Ford being born? The answer is in four parts. Ford sold about 650,000 fewer vehicles in the U.S. than GM last year, yet brought in slightly more revenue: $187.3 billion to $185 billion. The difference is mainly Ford Pro, the commercial arm that sells trucks and vans to businesses and governments earned $6.8 billion before interest and taxes in 2025 at a 10.3% margin. That’s more than double the earnings of Ford Blue, the gas and hybrid business, which made $3 billion, while the EV unit called Model e, lost $4.8 billion.

Kumar Galhotra, Ford’s chief operating officer, explained to Fortune how Ford’s next plan for Model e started with a skunkworks operation in California. “We tore down multiple Chinese OEM vehicles,” he said. “It was clear that because of the labor advantage and certain subsidies and battery areas, that their batteries are cheaper than ours.” So the team stopped trying to win on battery cost and designed around a smaller battery instead, with cleverer aerodynamics, fewer frictional losses and more efficient motors. “In the end, we’re pretty much equal in total system cost,” he said of Ford’s projection against China’s BYD, calling it “first principles thinking.”

The first vehicle on Ford’s new Universal EV platform is the Fathom, a $28,350 midsize electric pickup due in 2027. Ford says it will be able to send more than 10 kilowatts into a house during a blackout without the expensive wall-mounted inverter the F-150 Lightning needed. Asked how Ford makes money on that, a spokesperson said: “We see it as a compelling reason to buy Fathom.”

The rebirth includes a brand new business, Ford Energy, built to get a return on the billions Ford has sunk into batteries. By around March of 2025, veteran executive Lisa Drake could see that her battery plants just “weren’t full,” but Ford had already licensed lithium-iron-phosphate cells from China’s CATL and was building batteries in Marshall, Michigan. The same chemistry turned out to be close to ideal for energy storage for utilities and power developers racing to meet demand from data centers and an aging grid. “We actually were very fortunate,” Drake told Fortune. “I had a license for the best tech on the planet and the right tech for storage.”

In December, the board gave her $2 billion to start Ford Energy and she is retooling a Kentucky plant that used to make F-150 Lightning batteries, reusing about 30% to 40% of the equipment, with production set to begin in late 2027. The target customers are utilities, renewable energy developers and even data centers and hyperscalers, which don’t buy batteries directly but need power.

It’s part of Ford’s giant race against the calendar — Galhotra said Ford will end up “refreshing about 80% of our North American portfolio” between 2027 and 2029. And that’s where Rex Allen comes in. Every week saved in the clay studio will get to that refresh quicker.

Allen isn’t the only unlikely face of innovation at Ford. Over the river in Dearborn, another wears a giant Luigi-shaped mustache. His name is Greg Foresi.

The Ford Rouge Plant shows its age in a good way — a living museum, the first fully integrated auto factory in the world, still turning out cars in minutes through a century-old assembly line. And walking around, like a friendly engineer beat cop, is Foresi, the 3D-printing guy.

Foresi, who wears the horn-rimmed glasses of a Buddy Holly or a young Elvis Costello, got his signature look when he shaved off his eight-inch beard to play Luigi to a friend’s Mario one Halloween. When “mustache guy” shows up, he takes random requests from nearly everyone on the factory floor. That’s because he manages the 3D-printing lab, where roughly a dozen machines whirr away, steadily adding to nearly 300 tools and components in Foresi’s database, including about 50 individual socket covers.

“I’m out on the floor all day talking to people, trialing tools, evaluating equipment,” said the 35-year-old Foresi. Sometimes he gets five requests in one day, and sometimes nothing for five days. Foresi and his team point me toward a factory-floor wheel that isn’t black like the other ones, but a bright royal blue. This is a specialized part that filled a gap in availability during a supply-chain disruption. When Foresi saw the shortage coming, he printed his own.

Foresi explained that they didn’t have time to test the part. The choice was either trying the blue wheel and potentially creating a line stoppage — or not trying and definitely creating a stoppage. Some versions of the wheel failed, but Foresi said iterative improvements were made. “We avoided the problem entirely,” he said. Galhotra explained that Ford makes a new F-150 every 54 seconds at the plant, making Foresi’s blue wheel a hugely important component, but also not inherently scalable.

“One of the most radical things about 3D printing is that it decentralizes your logistics,” Foresi said. Galhotra put the limits plainly, saying that right now it’s “good for patches here and there,” like when a part is back-ordered or only can be flown in from somewhere else. Foresi agrees: “It’s not remotely close to replacing our conventional supply chain,” he said, adding that he gets “crazy requests” he has to turn down constantly. Some people think 3D printing is a joke, and other people “think that you can just 3D-print anything.”

Foresi had his own doubts at first. After stints at a supplier and a Ford stamping plant in Kansas City, he landed at the Dearborn truck plant as the only mechanical engineer in the final assembly department — ”a pretty intense job,” he said. “I’ve seen everything in the plant break and fixed everything,” Foresi said, recalling a line of people always waiting outside his cubicle. So when his talent was recognized as a fit for 3D printing (his “boss’ boss asked,” he said), his first instinct, like Allen’s, was fear for his job. His first instinct was that the job wouldn’t last, that 3D printing would lose favor and he’d be moved to a new role.

“I was worried that it was just a fad with the company and that it would lose energy and I would get laid off or something,” Foresi said. “They were like, ‘Too bad, you’re doing it anyway.’” Foresi hastened to add that he never felt pushed into taking the job or truly feared that he would be laid off; but he did think he might get reassigned. “After two or three months it became clear both to management and myself that printing was becoming so useful that a full-time position was needed.”

Mike Aragon and Alicia Boler Davis are both newcomers to Ford’s C-suite. The two Amazon veterans said have grown close, Aragon said, as their teams depend on each other and they bond over similar big-tech career transitions. Aragon recalls showing up for a leadership meeting five minutes late and CEO Jim Farley turning to him and saying, “Hey Mike, I want you to take on this AI initiative.” Boler Davis, a Detroiter who spent nearly 25 years working at GM before seven years out of manufacturing, said her return was like “drinking out of a firehose” and that she’s still “relearning the language, learning the culture.”

Boler Davis described the services and efficiencies that come from her use of smart data connected to a nationwide fleet and said she’s working toward a Ford Pro that can predict when a car will need a repair, or provide AI-enhanced advice to customers. As president of Ford Integrated Services, Aragon leads the teams that develop and scale digital products. “This is another reinvention on top of the reinvention that we already had to do,” he told Fortune.

At the Ford Accelerate event in Detroit in late September, Farley described to Fortune during a media briefing how Ford Pro actually uses AI to help dealership technicians, citing the example of an engine removal from a Ford Super Duty truck. “It’s like a two-day job” to take apart a Super Duty, he said, and AI is invaluable in walking customers through the process. “We are using AI for them to say, ‘Okay, do this, do that,’” he said. “They’re good mechanics, they just don’t know. They’ve never done it before.”

Galhotra was more measured about how far Ford has to go. There’s the AI that software companies talk about, on the one hand, and then “AI that’s going to change factories of how we build physical things, and in that second category, I haven’t seen really good execution yet. And even ourselves, we’re at the beginning of that journey.”

Boler Davis said some of Ford Pro’s fleet customers have pushed back. “We have some customers that, you know, don’t want you monitoring the vehicle or connected or don’t want to use the AI,” she said. Stressing that the cost of downtime can be hundreds, even thousands of dollars per vehicle on a given day, she said Ford Pro wants to empower fleet managers to help unlock the potential for their business. The tool isn’t to “get rid of the fleet manager,” she said, but to “make their job easier.”

Part of Ford’s pull for Aragon is personal. He said he remembers being driven to school in his grandfather’s 1963 F-100 truck. “I just remember the smell, which is like the smell of the nylon, and the dust — and then the Camels [cigarettes].” He said he’s planning to rebuild a model from the early ‘70s, which he’s planning to drive to work every day.

He’s not the only one at Ford with a project in the garage. Fairall restores old Volkswagens — he’s finished an ‘84 Westfalia Bus and is working on a ‘65 Split Window Bus. Allen is building his own car on the side, and he’s 3D-printing its front end. Asked whose intellectual property that makes it, Allen immediately responds, “It’s my car. “

Allen once feared the future enough to steer his son away from clay, though. Years ago, he brought his son, Derek, into a satellite studio and paid Derek to work six hours a day for him. “He started heading down this path,” Allen said, “he was getting pretty good at it. But I wanted him to be much more than just a modeler because I was worried about where modeling was going.” He steered him into engineering instead. “I always thought virtual reality would be there, but not in my lifetime,” he said. “I didn’t think I’d be part of it exactly.”

Allen says he thought he was ready to retire eight years ago, but now he’s excited to train the next generation of modelers in both virtual reality and clay. “I’m very excited about this whole chapter in my life,” he said. “I hop out of bed in the morning so I can come to work.”

Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news.

Baldwin Group CEO: The biggest AI disruption in insurance is the advisor

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence is going to disrupt insurance brokerage. There is not much question about that. The question that matters is what gets disrupted, and how differently firms are preparing for it.

Most firms are approaching AI the way they approached every prior wave of technology: take the work as it exists and do it faster. But AI is dramatically changing the economics of gathering information, comparing coverage and facilitating transactions. A firm whose value lives in those activities is competing in a race to the bottom.

A firm built on specialized expertise, deep client relationships and experiential human judgment can change the paradigm, turning risk into opportunity for clients. Firms that make that leap go from being a vendor of downside protection to a business partner that helps a client achieve the fullness of their ambition. Their value becomes constrained only by what their clients can achieve.

Showing up that way requires the fortitude to completely rebuild how the work gets done. At Baldwin, alongside our partners at Dell Family Office and Sequence Holdings, we have launched into that journey. We call it Refounding.

Consider a middle-market renewal: gather exposure data, prepare the submission, market it, compare quotes, build the proposal, and present. Weeks of cycle time, most of it waiting on the next hand-off. Apply AI to each step and you compress each one a little. Redesign the work around what AI does well and you compress the entire cycle, and you hand the recovered time back to the client advisor for the only parts a client will pay a premium for: understanding the business, anticipating what could go wrong and structuring solutions that unlock potential.

That is the work underway through Catalyst, our firm-wide transformation program. Over 17 weeks, we completed more than 47,000 AI-assisted tasks at quality rates above 98 percent while standardizing 27 processes in our Client Experience teams. Those numbers matter only for what they free up: capacity redeployed into advisory work, not simply banked as cost savings.

Three things happen when you make that shift. Cycle times compress meaningfully. Throughput rises because the same team carries more complexity. And complex knowledge work gets sharper. In one early claims-review application, AI identified coverage gaps in minutes that led to $71,000 in additional claim payment for a client. A professional still had to evaluate what the technology surfaced and decide what to do. That is the model: technology ingests and processes; the human provides the judgment; and experienced professionals remain accountable for every decision that carries real financial consequences for a client.

AI drives the marginal cost of information toward zero, and when information is free, the premium shifts to what you do with it. Clients will have more data than ever. What they will not have is someone who has seen 200 versions of their problem, who knows which carrier will actually show up when a claim is contested, and who will tell them the uncomfortable truth about a coverage gap before the loss rather than after. That is judgment, earned through experience. The relationship that lets a client hear that perspective and act on it with conviction is earned the same way.

It is also why insurance advice has to move earlier in the conversation. Too often, insurance enters the discussion after the consequential decisions about risk have been made. With AI compressing the mechanics, the best advisors will move upstream into the room where those decisions happen, helping clients understand how risk affects their investments, their capital and their growth. That is where the advisor’s value was always supposed to live. The transactional work got in the way.

For generations, early-career insurance professionals built judgment by doing the foundational work: reviewing policies, preparing submissions and sitting beside experienced colleagues. That apprenticeship was never efficient, but it worked. AI will eliminate the tasks it is built on. Remove those tasks and do nothing else, and we will produce a generation of advisors who can operate the tools but cannot challenge them. Judgment comes from working through hard situations, seeing the consequences of decisions and learning from people who have been there before. We have to redesign the apprenticeship for a world where the old on-ramp no longer exists.

At Baldwin, we are transforming how we work, and early-career colleagues are among the first to benefit. They now encounter real client challenges sooner than the traditional path allowed. We have begun retooling our internal university, The Azimuth Institute, using AI as a training environment where a new advisor can pressure-test a coverage program, or ask why a senior colleague structured a placement a certain way, and get a substantive answer in seconds. Because AI absorbs time-consuming tasks, these colleagues are now beginning to learn higher-level insurance concepts earlier and spend more time on client relationships and complex coverage questions. We are also committed to more structured mentorship, not less, because experienced advisors are best placed to teach how to rigorously interrogate what the technology produces. And we are rethinking performance: when a day’s work takes an hour, the question is no longer how fast a task was completed, but how well the day was spent on problem-solving and critical thinking for client solutions.

Five years from now, the role of the insurance advisor will look substantially different, and I expect the advisors who meet that moment to be more valuable than any generation before them, because what they bring is the one thing the technology cannot: experience, judgment and a relationship the client trusts.

The firms that win will not be the ones that focus on AI purely to drive efficiencies. They will be the ones that changed the work, then built the people who could do the new work well. That is the work in front of us, and we intend to get it done.

The opinions expressed in Fortune.com commentary pieces are solely the views of their authors and do not necessarily reflect the opinions and beliefs of Fortune.

Trevor Baldwin is the CEO of The Baldwin Group, a national advisory firm that delivers insurance, risk management, benefits and wealth management solutions. The Baldwin Group has agreed to be taken private by an investor group led by Sequence AI Holdings and DFO Management in a $32.50-a-share all-cash deal that is expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, pending stockholder and regulatory approvals. Dell Family Office and Sequence Holdings are named in this piece.

The 6 Most Hopeless Thriller TV Shows, Ranked

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

A hopeless thriller is one that keeps you grabbed by the neck but still ends in a painful manner. Someone in a thriller says, “We just need to find the truth,” and I immediately start worrying about them. Because finding the truth is one thing. Surviving what it does to your life is another. I've watched characters sacrifice everything to uncover a secret, only to discover that knowing the answer doesn't give them the slightest power to change it. It's infuriating. It's heartbreaking. And when a show earns that feeling, I cannot look away.

I think helplessness becomes truly frightening when you can understand every decision that leads somebody there. You wouldn't necessarily have behaved differently. You might have trusted the same person, followed the same evidence, or convinced yourself that one more sacrifice would finally put things right. These six thrillers are merciless about that very human tendency.

Sarah Linden (Mireille Enos) is preparing to leave the Seattle Police Department when the disappearance of teenager Rosie Larsen interrupts her plans. She works alongside detective Stephen Holder (Joel Kinnaman) and investigates a murder that tears through Rosie's family and becomes entangled with a political campaign. The Killing makes the investigation painfully personal, particularly for Rosie's parents, Stan (Brent Sexton) and Mitch (Michelle Forbes).

What gets under my skin is how little the truth can actually repair. Mitch can barely function inside the home where her daughter should still be living. Stan's grief keeps twisting into anger, leaving him vulnerable to decisions that could destroy what's left of his family. Meanwhile, Linden's inability to abandon the case damages her relationship with her own son. Everybody wants justice, but nobody can promise that justice will make living any easier. I find that distinction absolutely devastating.

Paul Spector (Jamie Dornan) has a wife, children, and a job as a grief counselor. He also stalks and murders women in Belfast. Detective Superintendent Stella Gibson (Gillian Anderson) arrives to investigate the killings, gradually closing in on a man who appears disturbingly ordinary outside his crimes. The Fall makes no secret of the murderer's identity. We know who he is, and the awful suspense comes from watching him move through a city full of people who don't.

Dornan's most disturbing moments aren't necessarily the violent ones. Sometimes Paul is simply behaving affectionately toward his children, and I want him as far away from them as possible. His domestic life offers camouflage without making his affection straightforwardly false, which is a sickening contradiction. Gibson is formidable, but intelligence and determination cannot undo what his victims have experienced. Even catching him means confronting the lasting damage he's inflicted. This is a thriller where the prospect of victory never feels remotely satisfying enough.

Mr. Robot begins with a thrilling fantasy of rebellion. Elliot Alderson (Rami Malek) works in cybersecurity while secretly hacking people and organizations he believes deserve exposing. When the mysterious Mr. Robot (Christian Slater) recruits him into a movement determined to destroy the financial power of E Corp, Elliot sees an opportunity to strike back at a system he despises. The movie gradually questions whether Elliot understands the consequences of his own actions.

I remember being almost embarrassingly excited about fsociety's ambitions. Then the economic damage starts spreading, ordinary people suffer, and the promised revolution becomes another nightmare. But Elliot's loneliness is even harder to endure. He can access almost anybody's private information while remaining terrified of genuine human intimacy. Sam Esmail, the show's creator, pushes that contradiction to its most painful extreme during Elliot's conversations with therapist Krista. The man determined to expose the world's hidden cruelty has spent years struggling to confront his own suffering. There is compassion here, thankfully, but very little comes without a terrible emotional cost.

Will Graham (Hugh Dancy) possesses a remarkable ability to understand how murderers think, making him invaluable to FBI investigator Jack Crawford (Laurence Fishburne). His increasingly fragile mental state leads him into therapy with Dr. Hannibal Lecter (Mads Mikkelsen), an extraordinarily sophisticated psychiatrist who happens to be a cannibalistic serial killer. Hannibal transforms their relationship into a psychological battle where affection, manipulation, and violence become almost impossible to separate.

I've never hated a fictional friendship quite so passionately. Hannibal recognizes Will's loneliness and offers him the intoxicating possibility of being completely understood. That attention feels almost loving, even as Hannibal deliberately destabilizes the person receiving it. Mikkelsen can turn a quiet dinner invitation into something sinister, while Dancy makes Will's desperate need for connection painfully convincing. The second season's confrontation at Hannibal's home is among the cruelest emotional disasters I've watched on television. What destroys me is that neither man can simply dismiss their attachment afterward. Understanding one another has become another form of imprisonment.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

A mysterious graphic novel called The Utopia Experiments draws a group of internet acquaintances into a conspiracy involving a ruthless organization known as the Network. Ian (Nathan Stewart-Jarrett), Becky (Alexandra Roach), Wilson Wilson (Adeel Akhtar), and young Grant (Oliver Woollford) find themselves pursued by killers who will do practically anything to obtain the manuscript. The original British Utopia turns their attempts to survive into an increasingly terrifying struggle against an organization whose influence seems limitless.

“Where is Jessica Hyde?” I swear, those four words become enough to make me nervous. Arby (Neil Maskell) asks the question with horrifying calm, as though killing people is simply an inconvenient part of his workday. The Network's resources are staggering, but the truly sickening part is its conviction that committing atrocities can be justified as serving humanity's future. Dennis Kelly's writing refuses to offer the comfort of uncomplicated heroes either. Survival gradually demands compromises that make the protagonists resemble the people they're fighting. The show was canceled after two seasons, leaving its extraordinary atmosphere of dread hanging over an unfinished story. Somehow, that feels appropriately cruel.

A young boy disappears in the German town of Winden, leading several families toward a mystery involving a network of caves, disturbing family secrets, and events separated by decades. Jonas Kahnwald (Louis Hofmann) becomes central to the investigation as Dark gradually establishes a world where time travel connects generations of people who barely understand the consequences of their own decisions.

Ulrich Nielsen (Oliver Masucci) is the character who really breaks my heart. His desperation to find his missing son pushes him toward an act he believes could prevent decades of suffering. Watching that certainty turn into something terrible is almost unbearable. But the show's bleakest idea extends far beyond one father's mistake. Parents damage children they haven't met yet. Lovers become trapped by histories they don't understand. Attempts to prevent catastrophes can help bring them about. Creators Baran bo Odar and Jantje Friese construct a world where love repeatedly becomes the reason people perpetuate suffering. And the cruelest part is that I never stop hoping alongside them. I want Jonas to save Martha. I want Ulrich to find his family. I want somebody, anybody, to escape the terrible inheritance passed between generations.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.
