Bitcoin falls below $70,000 as $870 million in bullish bets are liquidated

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Traders betting on higher Bitcoin prices faced nearly $870 million in forced closures over 24 hours as rate fears and NVIDIA uncertainty weighed on markets.

Bitcoin traded below $70,000 on Nov. 13 as heavy liquidations hit traders betting on higher prices, extending the crypto selloff against a backdrop of rate pressure and uncertainty at NVIDIA.

CoinGlass recorded $869.89 million in Bitcoin long liquidations over the last 24 hours, compared with $31 million in short liquidations. Over four hours, it recorded $559 million in long liquidations and $16 million in shorts.

By late Friday morning, UTC, Bitcoin was down to $69,224, with a rolling 24-hour low of $67,921.

The liquidation figures measure forced closures of Bitcoin positions tracked by the provider, a different measure from the change in Bitcoin's market capitalization.

Long positions benefit when prices rise. Their concentration in the liquidation figures is consistent with forced exits amplifying the decline as bullish bets come under pressure. The initial trigger remains unresolved, and position closures can involve different markets rather than a spot sale in every case.

The liquidation imbalance points to pressure on bullish positioning alongside the broader concerns about technology stocks and interest rates. Forced exits offer a plausible mechanism for amplifying the decline.

Rolling four hours to about 10:52 UTC, Nov. 13: $559.87M in long liquidations and $16.22M in shorts. This window is inside the 24-hour window.

The previous US stock session ended lower, though the broad indexes' closing losses were modest. The S&P 500 fell 0.35% to 7,592 on Nov. 12, according to LSEG data carried by the Financial Times. eOption's closing recap put the Nasdaq's decline at 0.39% and the Russell 2000's at 1.66%.

The broker also described a stronger dollar and rising Treasury yields as investors anticipated tighter Federal Reserve policy. That places Bitcoin's weakness within a wider retreat from risk.

NVIDIA's leadership change remained part of that backdrop. The company announced on Nov. 12 that Jensen Huang had resigned as president, CEO and director, with Colette Kress becoming interim CEO while retaining her CFO role. Its SEC filing dates the board's acceptance of his resignation to Nov. 11, effective immediately.

Rate concerns also predated Friday's crypto decline. In a Nov. 10 speech, Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he leaned toward a 25-basis-point December increase even if October monthly inflation improved. He said a core CPI reading around 0.3% or higher could warrant considering 50 basis points. That was his individual policy view, rather than an FOMC commitment.

Microsoft was also still working to restore affected services on Friday. Its 09:57 UTC update, reproduced in Penn's Microsoft incident feed, said recovery remained variable. There is no direct link between the outage and Bitcoin's decline, however, the risk-off mentality that comes with global technological uncertainty is clearly affecting the broader market.

For Bitcoin, the immediate picture is heavy pressure on bullish positions alongside a difficult macro and technology backdrop. NVIDIA's results remain scheduled for Nov. 17, giving investors another opportunity to assess the company's outlook under interim leadership.

How ‘Red Ink’ Pulled Off a Single-Take, 1940s Vampire Newsroom Thriller

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After the L.A. fires derailed their original debut feature, directors Benjamin J. Murray and Michael Christensen made a "monumental pivot" to a low-budget period movie shot as a oner.

When filmmakers Benjamin J. Murray and Michael Christensen set up their New York indie production outfit Incline Studios, the plan was to launch their slate with Blood Bank, a darkly satirical vampire heist film set in an alternative timeline, about an underground gang of dissidents who stage a daring raid on the city’s blood reserve.

Christensen’s script for Blood Bank imagined a world where, years before, a vampire virus infected the population. The government used the infection as an excuse to discriminate and scapegoat minority communities. The ambitious feature was deep into pre-production when the L.A. fires hit. Related Stories Movies 'Women and War' Follows Four Ukrainians' Journeys Towards Healing From Trauma (Exclusive IDFA Trailer) Movies 'Pop Pop' Dives Into Radicalization and Male Fragility in the Digital Age. Its Creator Says It's "More Topical Every Day" - and Urges Us to Join Hands.

“Several people involved lost their homes,” Christensen recalls. “We knew we had to shut the film down.”

Incline was already heavily invested, having spent significantly in upstate New York. The plan had been to shoot Blood Bank in Syracuse to take advantage of the state’s tax incentives. Rather than write off that investment, Murray and Christensen decided to make another movie there, only a lot cheaper and a lot faster. Their solution was to make a prequel, set in the same fictional world as Blood Bank, but with a couple of twists.

“It was a monumental pivot,” says Murray. “We knew we had to make the film in one location and for a fifth of the money and a fifth of the time [of Blood Bank]. But we wanted it to feel big. So we decided to make it a period film.”

They also took a page from Netflix hit Adolescence, deciding to shoot the film as a oner, unfolding in real time in a seemingly uninterrupted single take.

The result is Red Ink, which had its world premiere at the ToruńCamerimage festival this week. Co-directed by Murray and Christensen, from a script by Christensen and Murray serving as cinematographer, the film is a low-budget, noir-inspired newsroom thriller. With vampires.

Set over a single morning in November 1949, Red Ink unfolds inside the bustling headquarters of Verity Press, a New York Times-style newspaper. Billions actor Toby Leonard Moore stars as Bob Hoover, Verity‘s editor-in-chief, a celebrated war photographer whose Pulitzer-winning image of Nazi concentration camps helped sway public opinion at the end of World War II.

Now Hoover is about to break the biggest story of his career: A mysterious virus is sweeping the globe. Reports are emerging of infected people with a lust for blood and raw flesh, and bodies turning up with puncture marks on their necks.

With the paper’s deadline approaching, Hoover must decide how Verity will frame the story and, in turn, shape the national narrative. His publisher, Walter Nash (D.B. Sweeney), is under pressure from the politically ambitious Senator Conrad (Mike Doyle), who wants to exploit public fear to justify a government crackdown.

The eight-day shoot brought together an ensemble cast including Siobhan Fallon Hogan (Men in Black), Tommie Earl Jenkins (Wednesday), Eugene Cordero (Loki) and Oona Laurence (The Beguiled).

“We had three days prep and an eight day shoot,” says Christensen. “It was really a labor of love, for everyone involved.”

The filmmakers drew inspiration from classic thrillers of the period, with the 1952 Humphrey Bogart film Deadline USA, in which Bogart plays a newspaper editor fighting to save his publication, a particular touchstone.

For the technical execution, Murray turned to the same camera system used on Adolescence: the DJI Ronin 4D, which enables extended continuous camera movements across four axes, adding a vertical dimension to the traditional three-axis pan, tilt and roll of a gimbal. Although Red Ink plays as a single take, Murray assembled the final version from “around 13 shots of 8-10 minutes each.”

Red Ink wasn’t supposed to be Incline Studios’ first feature, but it serves as an appropriate calling card for the kind of movies the company wants to make: scrappy independent features that sit between IP-driven blockbusters and the darker, less accessible dramas that dominate the arthouse space. A post-credit sequence even sets up the world of Blood Bank, bringing the filmmakers back to the project that started it all.

Next on Incline’s slate is Engaged, an adventure comedy set in Malta that Murray compares in tone to Indiana Jones.

“We think there’s a gap in the independent space for movies of the kind we love,” says Murray, “fun, more mainstream films with original stories.”

“We want to break the mold, to bring validation back to the indie space,” adds Christensen. “I think with all the doom and gloom in the world, people are ready to feel hopeful again, at least at the movies.”

If you've secured a DMZ Blackmarket Locker key, here are all the locations at which you can use it

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You'll need to get yourself to one of the multiple MW4 DMZ Blackmarket Locker locations if you've found yourself in possession of a key for one, as it's the only way in. In contrast, if you've found a locker and want the goods inside, you might be wondering where to find the keys. Either way, I've got you covered below.

There are currently three Blackmarket Locker locations that I know about, and they're all marked on the map above, though you can see them in more detail in my more general guide to all MW4 DMZ keys. All of them are on the western side of the map, with two in the prison area and one in Deadtown.

Each location has two lockers in it, so if you've got two or more keys, you could take two with you each time to ensure you get a good look at everything on offer - just remember you can still only try and exfil with two MW4 guns. Alternatively, you could choose to just take one key with you and make the most of your weapon slots. I'd recommend taking a backpack with a secure slot and keeping your key in there until you use it, just in case.

Even without a key, these locations are great to check out as you can still find weapons and other valuable items in the locker room.

You can get keys for the Blackmarket Lockers in a couple of ways, one of which being finding them randomly by looting the map. A more reliable way of obtaining Blackmarket Locker keys is via Survival Kits that are unlocked through DMZ career progress, orders, and mission, as well as via the Battle Pass and events.

You'll acquire these Survival Kits, specifically Recon Equipment Kits, at levels 19 and 46 of your DMZ career. Once you've got them, you need to head to the Survival Kits menu in the DMZ lobby area, as seen in the image above, to claim them, at which point the items appear in your Stash.

Disney boss says talking to Hideo Kojima helped him fix a story issue in new movie Hexed

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disney animation boss Jared Bush reveals the unlikely source of inspiration behind overcoming a creative struggle on new movie Hexed – Hideo Kojima.

"True story… we were working on a section of #Hexed and there was a story point that was hard for me to crack," Bush, who also co-wrote Hexed, wrote on Twitter.

Bush added, "The inspiration for the fix came from talking to Hideo Kojima about an approach he took in Death Stranding 2. Games are cinema."

It's unclear exactly how Kojima indirectly contributed to the final product (Hexed isn't out for another few weeks, after all), but this doesn't mark the first time the legendary game developer has worked with Disney.

In the Japanese dub of Zootopia 2, Kojima had a bit-part as IT officer Paul Moldebrandt – and would go on to have multiple cameos in the next 12 months, including reprising his Cyberpunk role of Hideyoshi Oshima in Edgerunners season 2.

Hexed, starring Hailee Steinfeld as a fledgling witch, is Walt Disney Animation Studios' latest movie, following on from the billion-dollar hits of Moana 2 and Zootopia 2. But this marks the first original IP for the studio since 2023's Wish. The cast also stars Rashida Jones, Jodie Foster, Walton Goggins, Henry Winkler, and the live-action Beast himself, Dan Stevens.

Kojima, meanwhile, is busy at work on multiple game projects, including Physint and horror title O.D.
